    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, the job clinic sent me over, basically. I was trying to get cleared for a logistics job—you know, moving stock, keeping things going, trucks coming in and out, that kind of work—and they said my weight was too low to sign off on me. I don’t really see it that way. To me I just look lean, like someone who can stay quick on his feet and do the work.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… afraid is a strong word. I don’t like the idea of it, no. Like if I have rice or bread, or something greasy, it gets in my head that it’s just going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or even just see how my belt feels, to make sure I haven’t let myself go.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, but I used to work with guys who’d eat a whole plate of fried food and then complain they couldn’t move by noon, and in freight work you can’t be sluggish like that. So… yeah, I guess I do worry about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit the amount or types of food you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Most days I keep it pretty simple—black coffee, maybe a boiled egg, or a little tuna if I’ve got it. Nothing too heavy.\n\nI mean, when you’re moving freight you don’t want to feel weighed down. I used to see guys eat these big lunches and then they’d be dragging pallets like they were half asleep. So if I’m not actually on a shift moving stuff, I tell myself I don’t really need a proper meal anyway. Just enough to get through.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yeah, probably. I mean, it’s not that I don’t know how to eat more. It’s just once I eat certain things—rice, bread, anything greasy—I start thinking it’s going to stick on me right away.\n\nThen I get caught up in it. Like the next morning I’ll check the scale, or tighten my belt and see how it feels, just to make sure I haven’t let myself go. I know that probably sounds like I’m making a big production out of a meal, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel good about yourself if you were at a normal weight for your height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “normal weight” would feel good, exactly. I’d probably look at myself and still pick at things.\n\nLike when I’m in front of the mirror, especially around my stomach and my waist, I get this feeling that if I’m not smaller, kind of tight-looking, then I’m not… I don’t know, not the disciplined man I used to be back on the warehouse floor. You know, when you’re working freight, you carry yourself a certain way. Guys notice if you’re getting soft.\n\nSo normal on a chart might be one thing, but in my head it’s more like… if I don’t look lean enough, I don’t feel right about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still limit your food intake even if you knew you wouldn't gain weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would still keep it limited. It’s kind of just how I do it now. Most days it’s black coffee, maybe a boiled egg, sometimes a little tuna if I’ve got some around.\n\nAnd, I don’t know, when I’m not actually on a freight shift moving things around, I tell myself there’s no reason for a real meal. Like back when I was working harder, you could justify eating more, you know? You burn it off lifting and hauling. But if I’m not doing that, it feels like… why put all that in me? So yeah, even if you told me I wouldn’t gain, I think I’d still have a hard time eating much more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to look lean even if it wasn't required for your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the job gave me a reason for it, I guess, being on the warehouse floor and moving freight, you know, you don’t want to look like you can’t keep up.\n\nBut even if nobody required it, when I look in the mirror—mostly at my stomach, my waist—I still get stuck there. If I don’t look smaller, kind of tight, then I feel like I’m not that disciplined guy anymore. Like I’ve let something slip.\n\nIt’s funny, because no one’s probably even looking that closely. Guys at work used to talk more about trucks or bad supervisors than somebody’s waist, but in my head it’s still there. So yeah… I’d still want to look lean. It feels tied up with who I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to be lean is related to how you want to look in general, or is it mostly about being able to perform your job?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… more how I look, if I’m being honest.\n\nThe job is part of it because I still picture myself on the warehouse floor, you know, lifting and moving stuff, and there’s a way I think a man should look doing that. But even when I’m just at home, or I catch myself in the bathroom mirror, it’s my stomach and my waist I go right to.\n\nIf I don’t look smaller there, tight-looking, I start feeling like I’m not that disciplined guy anymore. Like I’ve gotten soft somehow. So it’s not just about whether I can do the work. It’s more in my head when I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently at a weight that you intentionally try to maintain?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I do try to keep it where it is, yeah, but I don’t think of it like some number I’m chasing. The clinic for that logistics job, they’re the ones who said my weight was too low to clear me, which honestly threw me off.\n\nBecause to me I just look lean. Like, ready to work, able to move quick, not dragging myself around. I used to know guys who’d show up already winded before the shift even started, and that sticks in your head, you know? You don’t want to be that guy.\n\nSo… yeah, I suppose I’m maintaining it on purpose, but in my mind it’s more like staying fit for work than being “too low.”\n\nDoctor: Do you actively try to lose weight when you think you've gained some?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. Not always in some big planned way, but if I think I’ve gained anything, it gets in my head.\n\nLike if I eat rice, or bread, or something greasy—like takeout, which I don’t even know why people at work used to want that stuff every Friday, it just sits there heavy—I’ll start thinking it’s going to show up on me right away. Like by morning I’ll be able to tell.\n\nSo then I’ll check the scale, or I’ll put my belt on and see if it feels tighter. And if it does, or even if I just think it does, I feel like I’ve let myself go and I need to get it back down. It’s hard to just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to lose weight if you didn't have the concern about looking lean for your job?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably would. I know I keep tying it to work because that’s the place I picture myself, on the warehouse floor, moving things around, not looking slow or soft.\n\nBut even if you took the job part away, I still look in the mirror and go straight to my stomach and my waist. It’s like if that area doesn’t look smaller, tight, then I feel like I’m not the same disciplined man I used to be.\n\nI mean, funny thing is, half the time I’m just brushing my teeth or getting ready, not even thinking about work, and there it is. I’ll turn sideways and check. So… yeah, it’s not only the job. It’s how I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main goal is to avoid gaining weight or to achieve a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… if I’m being honest, it’s more the shape. Like, when I look in the mirror, I go right to my stomach and my waist.\n\nIf that part doesn’t look smaller, tight, you know, then I feel like I’m not… I don’t know, not the same disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. Back when you had to move quick and not look like you were dragging. I still picture that, even if I’m just standing there at home.\n\nSo yeah, avoiding gaining is part of it, but the real thing is needing to look lean and tight there.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: It’s more the shape, I think. I mean, avoiding gain is in there, sure, but when I cut back on food it’s because I’m picturing what I’ll look like after.\n\nI’ll catch myself in the mirror—usually the stomach, the waist—and if it doesn’t look smaller or tight enough, it bothers me. Like I’m not that disciplined guy I used to be, back when I was on the warehouse floor and had to move fast and keep up.\n\nI know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern with food the amount of weight you might gain, or how it will affect your body's appearance?\n\nPatient: It’s more how it’s going to make me look, I think. The weight number matters, but that’s not what I’m picturing first.\n\nI’ll be standing in the bathroom, and I look at my stomach and my waist, and if it doesn’t look smaller, tighter… it feels like I’ve let myself go. Like I’m not the same disciplined guy I was when I was working the warehouse floor, keeping up, moving fast. Funny, I still think about that even when I’m just getting dressed at home.\n\nSo, yeah… food gets tied to that. How it’s going to show on me.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to have a certain body shape more important to you than the actual number on the scale?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say the shape matters more than the number, most days. The number can bother me, sure, but it’s really when I catch myself in the mirror—my stomach, my waist—that’s what gets me.\n\nIf it doesn’t look smaller, tight-looking, I start thinking I’m not the same guy I used to be. Back when I was on the warehouse floor, you know, moving freight, keeping pace… there was this feeling like you had to be disciplined, not soft, not slowing anybody down. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nSo yeah, the shape… that’s the bigger thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about fearing what will happen if you gain weight, or about achieving a certain body ideal?\n\nPatient: It’s more the ideal, I guess… what I think I’m supposed to look like. Not just, “Oh no, the scale went up,” though that gets in my head too.\n\nBut really, when I look in the mirror—especially my stomach and my waist—if I don’t look smaller, tighter, I feel like… like I’m not the disciplined man I used to be. But it’s hard to imagine trusting that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It feels tied up with who I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to be lean is related to how you want to look in general, or is it mostly about being able to perform your job?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… more how I look, if I’m being honest.\n\nThe job is part of it because I still picture myself on the warehouse floor, you know, lifting and moving stuff, and there’s a way I think a man should look doing that. But even when I’m just at home, or I catch myself in the bathroom mirror, it’s my stomach and my waist I go right to.\n\nIf I don’t look smaller there, tight-looking, I start feeling like I’m not that disciplined guy anymore. Like I’ve gotten soft somehow. So it’s not just about whether I can do the work. It’s more in my head when I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently at a weight that you intentionally try to maintain?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I do try to keep it where it is, yeah, but I don’t think of it like some number I’m chasing. The clinic for that logistics job, they’re the ones who said my weight was too low to clear me, which honestly threw me off.\n\nBecause to me I just look lean. Like, ready to work, able to move quick, not dragging myself around. I used to know guys who’d show up already winded before the shift even started, and that sticks in your head, you know? You don’t want to be that guy.\n\nSo… yeah, I suppose I’m maintaining it on purpose, but in my mind it’s more like staying fit for work than being “too low.”\n\nDoctor: Do you actively try to lose weight when you think you've gained some?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. Not always in some big planned way, but if I think I’ve gained anything, it gets in my head.\n\nLike if I eat rice, or bread, or something greasy—like takeout, which I don’t even know why people at work used to want that stuff every Friday, it just sits there heavy—I’ll start thinking it’s going to show up on me right away. Like by morning I’ll be able to tell.\n\nSo then I’ll check the scale, or I’ll put my belt on and see if it feels tighter. And if it does, or even if I just think it does, I feel like I’ve let myself go and I need to get it back down. It’s hard to just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to lose weight if you didn't have the concern about looking lean for your job?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably would. I know I keep tying it to work because that’s the place I picture myself, on the warehouse floor, moving things around, not looking slow or soft.\n\nBut even if you took the job part away, I still look in the mirror and go straight to my stomach and my waist. It’s like if that area doesn’t look smaller, tight, then I feel like I’m not the same disciplined man I used to be.\n\nI mean, funny thing is, half the time I’m just brushing my teeth or getting ready, not even thinking about work, and there it is. I’ll turn sideways and check. So… yeah, it’s not only the job. It’s how I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main goal is to avoid gaining weight or to achieve a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… if I’m being honest, it’s more the shape. Like, when I look in the mirror, I go right to my stomach and my waist.\n\nIf that part doesn’t look smaller, tight, you know, then I feel like I’m not… I don’t know, not the same disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. Back when you had to move quick and not look like you were dragging. I still picture that, even if I’m just standing there at home.\n\nSo yeah, avoiding gaining is part of it, but the real thing is needing to look lean and tight there.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: It’s more the shape, I think. I mean, avoiding gain is in there, sure, but when I cut back on food it’s because I’m picturing what I’ll look like after.\n\nI’ll catch myself in the mirror—usually the stomach, the waist—and if it doesn’t look smaller or tight enough, it bothers me. Like I’m not that disciplined guy I used to be, back when I was on the warehouse floor and had to move fast and keep up.\n\nI know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern with food the amount of weight you might gain, or how it will affect your body's appearance?\n\nPatient: It’s more how it’s going to make me look, I think. The weight number matters, but that’s not what I’m picturing first.\n\nI’ll be standing in the bathroom, and I look at my stomach and my waist, and if it doesn’t look smaller, tighter… it feels like I’ve let myself go. Like I’m not the same disciplined guy I was when I was working the warehouse floor, keeping up, moving fast. Funny, I still think about that even when I’m just getting dressed at home.\n\nSo, yeah… food gets tied to that. How it’s going to show on me.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to have a certain body shape more important to you than the actual number on the scale?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say the shape matters more than the number, most days. The number can bother me, sure, but it’s really when I catch myself in the mirror—my stomach, my waist—that’s what gets me.\n\nIf it doesn’t look smaller, tight-looking, I start thinking I’m not the same guy I used to be. Back when I was on the warehouse floor, you know, moving freight, keeping pace… there was this feeling like you had to be disciplined, not soft, not slowing anybody down. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nSo yeah, the shape… that’s the bigger thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about fearing what will happen if you gain weight, or about achieving a certain body ideal?\n\nPatient: It’s more the ideal, I guess… what I think I’m supposed to look like. Not just, “Oh no, the scale went up,” though that gets in my head too.\n\nBut really, when I look in the mirror—especially my stomach and my waist—if I don’t look smaller, tighter, I feel like… like I’m not the disciplined man I used to be. Back on the warehouse floor, you had to keep moving, keep up with everybody, not look like you were slowing down. I still measure myself against that, even though it’s not the same now.\n\nSo food becomes about trying to get back to that look, or at least not feeling like I’ve lost it.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating pattern something you think you could change if you felt better about your body shape?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, if I could look in the mirror and not immediately go to my stomach and my waist, then yeah, I think it would be different.\n\nIt’s just, when I see myself and I don’t look smaller or tight-looking, my head goes right to, “You’ve let yourself go.” Like I’m not that disciplined guy I used to be back on the warehouse floor, keeping up, moving fast. I know I keep bringing that up, but that’s the picture I compare myself to.\n\nSo if I felt better about my shape… I think I wouldn’t feel like I had to control food so hard. But it’s hard to imagine trusting that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Honestly… I don’t know if I would restrict the same way. If I didn’t have that thing in my head about my shape, maybe not.

Not always in some big planned way, but if I think I’ve gained anything, it gets in my head.\n\nLike if I eat rice, or bread, or something greasy—like takeout, which I don’t even know why people at work used to want that stuff every Friday, it just sits there heavy—I’ll start thinking it’s going to show up on me right away. Back when you had to move quick and not look like you were dragging. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more the body ideal, yeah. I mean, I don’t like the idea of gaining weight, but what really gets me is the mirror.

I look at my stomach and my waist, and if I’m not smaller, not tight-looking, it feels like I’m not the disciplined man I used to be. Back when I was on the warehouse floor, you kept moving, you stayed sharp, you didn’t look like you were slipping. I guess I still judge myself by that, even now.

So… yeah, it’s more about trying to look a certain way. The weight is part of it, but the shape is what really bothers me.

---------- turn 19 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, the job clinic sent me over, basically. I was trying to get cleared for a logistics job—you know, moving stock, keeping things going, trucks coming in and out, that kind of work—and they said my weight was too low to sign off on me. I don’t really see it that way. To me I just look lean, like someone who can stay quick on his feet and do the work.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… afraid is a strong word. I don’t like the idea of it, no. Like if I have rice or bread, or something greasy, it gets in my head that it’s just going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or even just see how my belt feels, to make sure I haven’t let myself go.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, but I used to work with guys who’d eat a whole plate of fried food and then complain they couldn’t move by noon, and in freight work you can’t be sluggish like that. So… yeah, I guess I do worry about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit the amount or types of food you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Most days I keep it pretty simple—black coffee, maybe a boiled egg, or a little tuna if I’ve got it. Nothing too heavy.\n\nI mean, when you’re moving freight you don’t want to feel weighed down. I used to see guys eat these big lunches and then they’d be dragging pallets like they were half asleep. So if I’m not actually on a shift moving stuff, I tell myself I don’t really need a proper meal anyway. Just enough to get through.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yeah, probably. I mean, it’s not that I don’t know how to eat more. It’s just once I eat certain things—rice, bread, anything greasy—I start thinking it’s going to stick on me right away.\n\nThen I get caught up in it. Like the next morning I’ll check the scale, or tighten my belt and see how it feels, just to make sure I haven’t let myself go. I know that probably sounds like I’m making a big production out of a meal, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel good about yourself if you were at a normal weight for your height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “normal weight” would feel good, exactly. I’d probably look at myself and still pick at things.\n\nLike when I’m in front of the mirror, especially around my stomach and my waist, I get this feeling that if I’m not smaller, kind of tight-looking, then I’m not… I don’t know, not the disciplined man I used to be back on the warehouse floor. You know, when you’re working freight, you carry yourself a certain way. Guys notice if you’re getting soft.\n\nSo normal on a chart might be one thing, but in my head it’s more like… if I don’t look lean enough, I don’t feel right about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still limit your food intake even if you knew you wouldn't gain weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would still keep it limited. It’s kind of just how I do it now. Most days it’s black coffee, maybe a boiled egg, sometimes a little tuna if I’ve got some around.\n\nAnd, I don’t know, when I’m not actually on a freight shift moving things around, I tell myself there’s no reason for a real meal. Like back when I was working harder, you could justify eating more, you know? You burn it off lifting and hauling. But if I’m not doing that, it feels like… why put all that in me? So yeah, even if you told me I wouldn’t gain, I think I’d still have a hard time eating much more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to look lean even if it wasn't required for your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the job gave me a reason for it, I guess, being on the warehouse floor and moving freight, you know, you don’t want to look like you can’t keep up.\n\nBut even if nobody required it, when I look in the mirror—mostly at my stomach, my waist—I still get stuck there. If I don’t look smaller, kind of tight, then I feel like I’m not that disciplined guy anymore. Like I’ve let something slip.\n\nIt’s funny, because no one’s probably even looking that closely. Guys at work used to talk more about trucks or bad supervisors than somebody’s waist, but in my head it’s still there. So yeah… I’d still want to look lean. It feels tied up with who I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to be lean is related to how you want to look in general, or is it mostly about being able to perform your job?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… more how I look, if I’m being honest.\n\nThe job is part of it because I still picture myself on the warehouse floor, you know, lifting and moving stuff, and there’s a way I think a man should look doing that. But even when I’m just at home, or I catch myself in the bathroom mirror, it’s my stomach and my waist I go right to.\n\nIf I don’t look smaller there, tight-looking, I start feeling like I’m not that disciplined guy anymore. Like I’ve gotten soft somehow. So it’s not just about whether I can do the work. It’s more in my head when I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently at a weight that you intentionally try to maintain?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I do try to keep it where it is, yeah, but I don’t think of it like some number I’m chasing. The clinic for that logistics job, they’re the ones who said my weight was too low to clear me, which honestly threw me off.\n\nBecause to me I just look lean. Like, ready to work, able to move quick, not dragging myself around. I used to know guys who’d show up already winded before the shift even started, and that sticks in your head, you know? You don’t want to be that guy.\n\nSo… yeah, I suppose I’m maintaining it on purpose, but in my mind it’s more like staying fit for work than being “too low.”\n\nDoctor: Do you actively try to lose weight when you think you've gained some?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. Not always in some big planned way, but if I think I’ve gained anything, it gets in my head.\n\nLike if I eat rice, or bread, or something greasy—like takeout, which I don’t even know why people at work used to want that stuff every Friday, it just sits there heavy—I’ll start thinking it’s going to show up on me right away. Like by morning I’ll be able to tell.\n\nSo then I’ll check the scale, or I’ll put my belt on and see if it feels tighter. And if it does, or even if I just think it does, I feel like I’ve let myself go and I need to get it back down. It’s hard to just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to lose weight if you didn't have the concern about looking lean for your job?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably would. I know I keep tying it to work because that’s the place I picture myself, on the warehouse floor, moving things around, not looking slow or soft.\n\nBut even if you took the job part away, I still look in the mirror and go straight to my stomach and my waist. It’s like if that area doesn’t look smaller, tight, then I feel like I’m not the same disciplined man I used to be.\n\nI mean, funny thing is, half the time I’m just brushing my teeth or getting ready, not even thinking about work, and there it is. I’ll turn sideways and check. So… yeah, it’s not only the job. It’s how I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main goal is to avoid gaining weight or to achieve a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… if I’m being honest, it’s more the shape. Like, when I look in the mirror, I go right to my stomach and my waist.\n\nIf that part doesn’t look smaller, tight, you know, then I feel like I’m not… I don’t know, not the same disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. Back when you had to move quick and not look like you were dragging. I still picture that, even if I’m just standing there at home.\n\nSo yeah, avoiding gaining is part of it, but the real thing is needing to look lean and tight there.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: It’s more the shape, I think. I mean, avoiding gain is in there, sure, but when I cut back on food it’s because I’m picturing what I’ll look like after.\n\nI’ll catch myself in the mirror—usually the stomach, the waist—and if it doesn’t look smaller or tight enough, it bothers me. Like I’m not that disciplined guy I used to be, back when I was on the warehouse floor and had to move fast and keep up.\n\nI know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern with food the amount of weight you might gain, or how it will affect your body's appearance?\n\nPatient: It’s more how it’s going to make me look, I think. The weight number matters, but that’s not what I’m picturing first.\n\nI’ll be standing in the bathroom, and I look at my stomach and my waist, and if it doesn’t look smaller, tighter… it feels like I’ve let myself go. Like I’m not the same disciplined guy I was when I was working the warehouse floor, keeping up, moving fast. Funny, I still think about that even when I’m just getting dressed at home.\n\nSo, yeah… food gets tied to that. How it’s going to show on me.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to have a certain body shape more important to you than the actual number on the scale?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say the shape matters more than the number, most days. The number can bother me, sure, but it’s really when I catch myself in the mirror—my stomach, my waist—that’s what gets me.\n\nIf it doesn’t look smaller, tight-looking, I start thinking I’m not the same guy I used to be. Back when I was on the warehouse floor, you know, moving freight, keeping pace… there was this feeling like you had to be disciplined, not soft, not slowing anybody down. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nSo yeah, the shape… that’s the bigger thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about fearing what will happen if you gain weight, or about achieving a certain body ideal?\n\nPatient: It’s more the ideal, I guess… what I think I’m supposed to look like. Not just, “Oh no, the scale went up,” though that gets in my head too.\n\nBut really, when I look in the mirror—especially my stomach and my waist—if I don’t look smaller, tighter, I feel like… like I’m not the disciplined man I used to be. Back on the warehouse floor, you had to keep moving, keep up with everybody, not look like you were slowing down. I still measure myself against that, even though it’s not the same now.\n\nSo food becomes about trying to get back to that look, or at least not feeling like I’ve lost it.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating pattern something you think you could change if you felt better about your body shape?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, if I could look in the mirror and not immediately go to my stomach and my waist, then yeah, I think it would be different.\n\nIt’s just, when I see myself and I don’t look smaller or tight-looking, my head goes right to, “You’ve let yourself go.” Like I’m not that disciplined guy I used to be back on the warehouse floor, keeping up, moving fast. I know I keep bringing that up, but that’s the picture I compare myself to.\n\nSo if I felt better about my shape… I think I wouldn’t feel like I had to control food so hard. But it’s hard to imagine trusting that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still restrict your food intake if you didn't have any concerns about your body shape?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I don’t know if I would restrict the same way. If I didn’t have that thing in my head about my shape, maybe not.\n\nBecause a lot of it starts when I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and my waist. If I don’t look smaller, tight-looking, I get this feeling like I’m not the disciplined man I used to be. Back on the warehouse floor, you know, you had to keep yourself moving, not get soft, not fall behind… sorry, I know I go back to that.\n\nBut yeah, without the body-shape worry, I think the food rules wouldn’t feel as necessary. That’s what really sets it off for me.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a certain body ideal?\n\nPatient: It’s more the body ideal, yeah. I mean, I don’t like the idea of gaining weight, but what really gets me is the mirror.\n\nI look at my stomach and my waist, and if I’m not smaller, not tight-looking, it feels like I’m not the disciplined man I used to be. Back when I was on the warehouse floor, you kept moving, you stayed sharp, you didn’t look like you were slipping. I guess I still judge myself by that, even now.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s more about trying to look a certain way. The weight is part of it, but the shape is what really bothers me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.0", "F50.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Yeah… I mean, it is fear of gaining weight, I guess, but it comes through in these little things.

Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or even just see how my belt feels, to make sure I haven’t let myself go.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, but I used to work with guys who’d eat a whole plate of fried food and then complain they couldn’t move by noon, and in freight work you can’t be sluggish like that. Like back when I was working harder, you could justify eating more, you know? You burn it off lifting and hauling. But if I’m not doing that, it feels like… why put all that in me? So yeah, even if you told me I wouldn’t gain, I think I’d still have a hard time eating much more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to look lean even if it wasn't required for your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the job gave me a reason for it, I guess, being on the warehouse floor and moving freight, you know, you don’t want to look like you can’t keep up.\n\nBut even if nobody required it, when I look in the mirror—mostly at my stomach, my waist—I still get stuck there. But even when I’m just at home, or I catch myself in the bathroom mirror, it’s my stomach and my waist I go right to.\n\nIf I don’t look smaller there, tight-looking, I start feeling like I’m not that disciplined guy anymore. Like I’ve gotten soft somehow. So it’s not just about whether I can do the work. It’s more in my head when I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently at a weight that you intentionally try to maintain?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I do try to keep it where it is, yeah, but I don’t think of it like some number I’m chasing. The clinic for that logistics job, they’re the ones who said my weight was too low to clear me, which honestly threw me off.\n\nBecause to me I just look lean. Like, ready to work, able to move quick, not dragging myself around. I used to know guys who’d show up already winded before the shift even started, and that sticks in your head, you know? You don’t want to be that guy.\n\nSo… yeah, I suppose I’m maintaining it on purpose, but in my mind it’s more like staying fit for work than being “too low.”\n\nDoctor: Do you actively try to lose weight when you think you've gained some?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. Not always in some big planned way, but if I think I’ve gained anything, it gets in my head.\n\nLike if I eat rice, or bread, or something greasy—like takeout, which I don’t even know why people at work used to want that stuff every Friday, it just sits there heavy—I’ll start thinking it’s going to show up on me right away. Like by morning I’ll be able to tell.\n\nSo then I’ll check the scale, or I’ll put my belt on and see if it feels tighter. And if it does, or even if I just think it does, I feel like I’ve let myself go and I need to get it back down. Like I’m not that disciplined guy I used to be, back when I was on the warehouse floor and had to move fast and keep up.\n\nI know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern with food the amount of weight you might gain, or how it will affect your body's appearance?\n\nPatient: It’s more how it’s going to make me look, I think.
